# Lever responses: Iceland Inc. (`iceland`)

Generated by `bun run levers`. Every lever is moved hard, one at a time, and held for 240 months (a one-off fires once), in each stabiliser mode (Manual, Automatic): 222 runs. The lever is set before month 1 is computed (event t = 0), so month 1 is the first month it acts. **Every effect is the difference from the no-change run in the same mode** (the same engine and mode, no lever event), month by month; nothing is measured from month 0.

Columns m1 … m240 are the effect in those months; *Peak* is the largest effect in absolute value and its month; *Long run* is the mean effect over the final 12 months. Effects below 0.005 show as 0. Each variable's unit is in brackets after its name:

- **%**: the percent difference from the no-change run’s level in the same month.
- **pp**: the difference in percentage points of a rate or a share (a rate of 4% against 3% is +1 pp).
- **% (+ stronger)**: the percent difference from the no-change run’s level; positive is a stronger króna.
- **pp of GDP**: the difference, in percentage points, of a ratio to nominal GDP: this month’s GDP at an annual rate, or GDP over the past 12 months, as the variable’s definition says. A ratio does not grow with the price level.

Kinks not traced: `mortgageTargetY`, `mortgageTargetW`, `exportVolumeFish`, `exportVolumeAluminium`, `exportVolumeTourism`, `exportVolumeOther`, `employmentFC`, `employmentFR`, `employmentXF`, `employmentXA`, `employmentXT`, `employmentXO` combine their terms non-additively (a min, a max, a cap) but carry no regime label, so the Regimes and Flicker flags cannot show when they bind or switch.

The JSON file beside this one has the same data, every indicator at the same horizons, and the thresholds. How to read and vet the report: `docs/authoring.md`, “Vetting levers”.

## Flags

| Flag | Meaning and threshold |
|---|---|
| Non-finite | A variable or stock became NaN or infinite. |
| Residual | An accounting check's residual exceeded 1e-9. |
| Sign | A position took the wrong sign for its role (CheckReport.signViolations): an overdrawn asset or a liability turned into a claim. |
| Implausible | A variable broke an economic bound of the harness (plausibility.ts): unemployment outside [0, 50%], a price index at or below zero, a negative key rate. |
| Extreme | A headline moved further than any routine policy change should move it: a level by more than 50% of its no-change value, or a rate or ratio by more than 25 pp, in some month. Usually a runaway nominal path; check it before anything else in the run. |
| Policy moved | On Manual, a policy instrument whose own levers were not moved differs from the no-change run by more than 1e-9 (model units). |
| Month-1 jump | A headline that should adjust gradually has 75% or more of its peak effect already in month 1 (peak at least 0.05). |
| Sawtooth | In the first 60 months, 4 or more sign alternations in a row of month-to-month changes, each above max(0.001, 1% of the peak). |
| Unsettled | Still moving at the end: the effect changed by more than max(0.02, 2% of the peak) over the final 12 months. |
| Explosive | Unsettled, and the final effect is at least 2× the largest effect in the first half of the run and at least 1. |
| Asymmetry | At month 12, the effects per unit of lever of the moderate up and down steps differ in sign or by more than 3× (larger effect at least 0.05). Caps and floors that bind one way are the usual cause. |
| Mode sign | At month 12, Manual and Automatic move a non-policy headline in opposite directions (each at least 0.02). |
| Flicker | A rule's regime label changed 6 or more times within 60 months: a floor or cap switching on and off. |
| Inert | No run of the lever moves any headline or indicator by 0.005 or more in any month. Either the lever needs another shock to act on (then the report also runs it with a declared companion shock), or it is not wired to anything. |
| Regimes | At least one rule ran in a different regime (a floor, cap or limit binding or released) from the no-change run in the same month. Informational: it shows what drives the result. |

Policy instruments checked on Manual: the key rate (`keyRate`, moved only by `keyRateFixed` or `keyRateAddon`); the income-tax rate (`taxRate`, moved only by `incomeTax` or `incomeTaxOffset`); the VAT rate (`vatRate`, moved only by `vat`).

## No-change runs

- Manual: largest move of a headline from its baseline 6.66e-12 (display units). Flags: none.
- Automatic: largest move of a headline from its baseline 4.73e-12 (display units). Flags: none.

## Key interest rate (`keyRateFixed`)

*Setting, unit %, default 3, range 0 to 15 in steps of 0.25.*

The central bank’s key interest rate, held where you set it. The central bank’s inflation rule only suggests a rate beside the lever.

**Definition.** Level of the key rate in percent a year, applied in the month it is set and held there until you change it (stabilisers on Manual). Nothing in the model moves it. The default, 3%, is the neutral rate, so the baseline is unchanged. A rate held far above it for many years with taxes and spending also held (Manual) ends up raising spending: the government’s interest bill grows with its debt, and that interest is income for households and pension funds (at 15%, output is back above baseline after about seven years; decision 0002 §6). It has no effect while stabilisers are Automatic, when the rule sets the key rate.

Runs: 0 % (min); 2.25 % (down); 6 % (up); 15 % (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Not run on Automatic: the lever is shown only on Manual (showWhen).

## Key rate: your offset to the rule (`keyRateAddon`)

*Setting, unit pp, default 0, range -3 to 5 in steps of 0.25.*

Sets the key rate this many points above (or below) what the central bank’s inflation rule says.

**Definition.** Level shift in the key rate, in percentage points on top of the rule’s rate, applied in the month it is set and persistent while set (stabilisers on Automatic). The rule keeps reacting to inflation and output underneath it. A lasting offset acts partly like a lower inflation target (inflation stays about 0.2 pp below baseline per point) and partly as a lasting drag on output (about 0.3% per point after 20 years): expectations are only half anchored, and a higher rate moves interest income between borrowers and savers for good. That lasting output effect is a stock-flow departure from long-run neutrality, like the one a rate held high on Manual shows (decision 0002 §6). Setting it back to 0 returns the key rate to the rule’s rate that month. It has no effect while stabilisers are Manual.

Runs: -3 pp (min); -0.75 pp (down); 1.25 pp (up); 5 pp (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Not run on Manual: the lever is shown only on Automatic (showWhen).

## Bank lending appetite (`lendingAppetite`)

*Setting, unit % of GDP per yr, default 0, range -3 to 3 in steps of 0.25.*

Extra (or less) mortgage lending banks are willing to push each year.

**Definition.** Level shift in households’ desired new mortgage borrowing, % of baseline GDP a year, split between the young and working age by their share of mortgage debt; persistent while set and still subject to the debt-service and loan-to-value caps. Setting it back to 0 ends the push; loans already made are repaid over their term.

Runs: -3 % of GDP per yr (min); -0.75 % of GDP per yr (down); 0.75 % of GDP per yr (up); 3 % of GDP per yr (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Mode sign**: -3 % of GDP per yr (min): Government balance -0.07 on Manual, 0.26 on Automatic; Profits, exporters (real) -0.27 on Manual, 1.17 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -0.75 % of GDP per yr (down): Government balance -0.02 on Manual, 0.08 on Automatic; Profits, exporters (real) -0.08 on Manual, 0.37 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 0.75 % of GDP per yr (up): Government balance 0.02 on Manual, -0.08 on Automatic; Profits, exporters (real) 0.07 on Manual, -0.38 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 3 % of GDP per yr (max): Government balance 0.05 on Manual, -0.17 on Automatic; Profits, exporters (real) 0.14 on Manual, -0.83 on Automatic.

## Debt-service cap (`dstiCap`)

*Setting, unit pp, default 0, range -15 to 15 in steps of 1.*

Shifts the payment-to-income caps on new mortgages (40% for first-time buyers, 35% for others).

**Definition.** Level shift in both debt-service caps, in percentage points of income, applied to new lending at once and persistent while set. Existing loans are unaffected. Setting it back to 0 restores the Rules 1300/2025 caps.

Runs: -15 pp (min); -4 pp (down); 4 pp (up); 15 pp (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

## Loan-to-value cap (`ltvCap`)

*Setting, unit %, default 0, range 0 to 100 in steps of 5.*

0 = off. Otherwise new mortgages may pay for at most this share of the value of the homes bought this year (first-time buyers 10 points more).

**Definition.** Level of the loan-to-value cap in percent, applied to new lending at once and persistent while set; 0 switches it off. It is the largest ratio of new mortgage debt to the market value of the homes a group buys this year, over and above loans that replace repayments. The existing stock of loans is never tested, so loans already made are unaffected.

Runs: 25 % (up); 100 % (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Also run on top of a companion shock, **Bank lending appetite 3 % of GDP per yr** (`lendingAppetite`), because the cap applies only to new lending beyond what replaces repayments, and there is none at baseline. Those runs are measured against the run with the companion shock alone, in the same mode, and follow the plain runs.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Inert**: none of its 4 runs moves a headline or an indicator by 0.005 or more in any month.
- **Mode sign** (with the companion shock): 25 % (up): Government balance -0.03 on Manual, 0.11 on Automatic; Profits, exporters (real) -0.09 on Manual, 0.51 on Automatic.

## Foreign demand (`foreignDemand`)

*Setting, unit %, default 0, range -20 to 20 in steps of 1.*

Demand abroad for Icelandic goods and services other than tourism and aluminium: it moves other exporters one for one and fisheries lightly.

**Definition.** Level shift in foreign demand, in percent of baseline, persistent while set. It reaches export volumes over a few quarters (about a fifth in the first month, 95% within a year): other exporters’ volume moves by the full percentage and marine volume by 0.3 of it (catches are capped by quotas). Tourism has its own lever and the smelters run at capacity. Setting it back to 0 returns demand to baseline the same way.

Runs: -20 % (min); -5 % (down); 5 % (up); 20 % (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Mode sign**: -20 % (min): Inflation (12-month CPI) -0.15 on Manual, 0.05 on Automatic; Consumer price level -0.15 on Manual, 0.05 on Automatic; Household consumption (real) -0.42 on Manual, 0.13 on Automatic; Real house prices -0.95 on Manual, 0.17 on Automatic; Government balance -0.44 on Manual, 0.29 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -5 % (down): Household consumption (real) -0.11 on Manual, 0.03 on Automatic; Real house prices -0.24 on Manual, 0.04 on Automatic; Government balance -0.11 on Manual, 0.07 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 5 % (up): Household consumption (real) 0.11 on Manual, -0.03 on Automatic; Real house prices 0.23 on Manual, -0.06 on Automatic; Government balance 0.11 on Manual, -0.06 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 20 % (max): Inflation (12-month CPI) 0.10 on Manual, -0.09 on Automatic; Consumer price level 0.10 on Manual, -0.09 on Automatic; Household consumption (real) 0.44 on Manual, -0.11 on Automatic; Real house prices 0.90 on Manual, -0.22 on Automatic; Government balance 0.43 on Manual, -0.25 on Automatic.

## Tourism (`tourism`)

*Setting, unit %, default 0, range -60 to 30 in steps of 5.*

Foreign visitors’ spending: it drives the tourism sector.

**Definition.** Level shift in tourism export volume (what foreign visitors buy), in percent of baseline, persistent while set. A rise reaches visitor numbers over a few quarters (95% within a year), limited by flights, hotel rooms and staff; a fall hits within a month or so, as in 2010 and 2020. Tourism firms’ revenue, jobs and imports follow. Setting it back to 0 ends it the same way.

Runs: -60 % (min); -15 % (down); 10 % (up); 30 % (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Asymmetry** (Automatic): Household consumption (real) -0.06 for 10 vs -0.02 for -15 (same direction); Profits, exporters (real) 1.48 for 10 vs 0.18 for -15 (same direction).
- **Mode sign**: -60 % (min): Inflation (12-month CPI) -0.64 on Manual, 0.13 on Automatic; Consumer price level -0.64 on Manual, 0.13 on Automatic; Bank capital ratio 0.07 on Manual, -0.18 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -15 % (down): Inflation (12-month CPI) -0.11 on Manual, 0.14 on Automatic; Consumer price level -0.11 on Manual, 0.14 on Automatic; Investment (real) -0.58 on Manual, 0.13 on Automatic; Real house prices -1.20 on Manual, 0.10 on Automatic; Government balance -0.42 on Manual, 0.21 on Automatic; and 1 more.
- **Mode sign**: 10 % (up): Inflation (12-month CPI) 0.07 on Manual, -0.03 on Automatic; Consumer price level 0.07 on Manual, -0.03 on Automatic; Household consumption (real) 0.23 on Manual, -0.06 on Automatic; Investment (real) 0.23 on Manual, -0.05 on Automatic; Real house prices 0.51 on Manual, -0.07 on Automatic; and 1 more.

## Króna sentiment shock (`kronaShock`)

*One-off, unit %, default -10, range -25 to 25 in steps of 1.*

A one-off shift in what investors think the króna is worth. Negative means a weaker króna.

**Definition.** One-off shift in the króna’s target value by this percentage (−10: a target 10% weaker), fired once. The króna falls by most of it within a quarter (−10: about 8% by month 3). Then the rate gap and non-residents’ holdings pull it back, so about half the fall is gone after a year (about 4% weaker at month 12) and most of it after two. The shift in the target itself fades at about 10% of its size a year. Prices, rates and trade respond.

Runs: -25 % (min); -10 % (default); -5 % (half); 5 % (-half); 10 % (-default); 25 % (max). A one-off fires once, before month 1.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Asymmetry** (Manual): Real house prices 0 for 5 vs -0.11 for -5 (162.7×).
- **Mode sign**: -25 % (min): Mortgage debt / GDP 0.44 on Manual, -0.14 on Automatic; Government debt / GDP -0.47 on Manual, 0.88 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -10 % (default): Mortgage debt / GDP 0.19 on Manual, -0.02 on Automatic; Government debt / GDP -0.17 on Manual, 0.33 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -5 % (half): Government debt / GDP -0.08 on Manual, 0.16 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 5 % (-half): Government debt / GDP 0.08 on Manual, -0.16 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 10 % (-default): Government debt / GDP 0.14 on Manual, -0.30 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 25 % (max): Government debt / GDP 0.32 on Manual, -0.69 on Automatic.

## Foreign interest rate (`foreignRate`)

*Setting, unit pp, default 0, range -3 to 5 in steps of 0.25.*

Interest rates abroad; a higher rate pulls carry money and pension savings out of krónur, so the króna weakens.

**Definition.** Level shift in the foreign interest rate, in percentage points, applied at once and persistent while set. The rate gap with abroad narrows, so carry traders sell króna bonds and pension funds raise their foreign target by 1 point of assets per point: the króna weakens for the first three years (about 0.9% on average over the first two per point on Automatic). It also raises the yield on the central bank’s reserves and on the funds’ foreign bonds (not their shares), and so the profit the central bank hands to the government. That extra income, spent at home, strengthens the króna slowly: after about three and a half years it is stronger than at the start, and the drift does not level off. Per point held, the króna is about 2–2.5% stronger after ten years and 3.5–4% after twenty on Automatic (3–3.5% and 8–10.5% on Manual), and the price level about 1.2% lower after twenty years (5% on Manual), with inflation still below baseline. This is a known gap (decision 0002 §6): the model has no steady state with a lasting surplus of foreign income. Setting it back to 0 ends it.

Runs: -3 pp (min); -0.75 pp (down); 1.25 pp (up); 5 pp (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

## World prices (`importPrices`)

*Setting, unit %, default 0, range -20 to 40 in steps of 1.*

Foreign-currency prices of imports and of fish and aluminium.

**Definition.** Level shift in world prices in foreign currency, in percent, applied at once and persistent while set. Fish and aluminium revenue in krónur jumps at once and import prices in krónur follow within a year or two. What buyers pay for imported goods rises by less, since part of it is the Icelandic cost of getting the goods to them: +10 raises consumer prices about 2% within a year and 2.8% within two on Automatic. The króna strengthens only slowly, over several years, as purchasing-power parity absorbs the new world prices, which takes back part of the rise in krónur. Setting it back to 0 ends it.

Runs: -20 % (min); -5 % (down); 10 % (up); 40 % (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Asymmetry** (Manual): Real house prices -0.32 for 10 vs 0.04 for -5 (3.6×).
- **Mode sign**: -20 % (min): Government debt / GDP 0.27 on Manual, -0.88 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -5 % (down): Government debt / GDP 0.07 on Manual, -0.24 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 10 % (up): Government debt / GDP -0.14 on Manual, 0.45 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 40 % (max): Unemployment rate -0.23 on Manual, 0.03 on Automatic; Government debt / GDP -0.58 on Manual, 1.65 on Automatic.

## World fish prices (`fishPrices`)

*Setting, unit %, default 0, range -30 to 30 in steps of 1.*

What foreign buyers pay for Icelandic fish, in foreign currency.

**Definition.** Level shift in the world price of marine products, in percent, on top of the world-prices lever; applied at once and persistent while set. Quotas cap the catch, so most of the change goes into fisheries’ revenue and profit; volume moves only a little (about 3% at +30), through fuller use of quotas, the product mix and aquaculture, and the stronger króna that follows takes back part of the gain. A third of fisheries’ profit above normal goes to the state as the fishing fee two years later. Setting it back to 0 ends it.

Runs: -30 % (min); -8 % (down); 8 % (up); 30 % (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

## World aluminium price (`aluminiumPrice`)

*Setting, unit %, default 0, range -40 to 40 in steps of 1.*

The aluminium price on world markets, in dollars.

**Definition.** Level shift in the world aluminium price, in percent, on top of the world-prices lever; applied at once and persistent while set. The smelters produce at capacity, so revenue moves one for one while their alumina bill does not, and their foreign owners take almost all of the extra profit. Setting it back to 0 ends it.

Runs: -40 % (min); -10 % (down); 10 % (up); 40 % (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

## Wage settlement (`wageSettlement`)

*One-off, unit %, default 10, range -5 to 20 in steps of 0.5.*

A one-off jump in nominal wage rates, as after a collective agreement.

**Definition.** One-off level shift: the wage rate, private and public, jumps by this percentage in the month the lever is fired. It is not reversed; afterwards wages follow the Phillips curve. Firms price the pay rise in within a few months, so at first the real-wage gain erodes mainly through prices (+10% on Manual: consumer prices about 2.8% higher after a year, wages about 1.9 points below their new level). Wage bargainers then work the rest off over the following rounds (the error correction, with a lag of about a year), until wages are back in line with what firms earn per unit of value added. With labour about half of unit cost, domestic prices rise by only about two-thirds of the wage rise at a given exchange rate, so in the end wages give back most of the settlement: on Automatic the price level is about 3.3% higher after six years and about 2.5% after twenty, with wages a little above it.

Runs: -5 % (min, -half); 5 % (half); 10 % (default); 20 % (max). A one-off fires once, before month 1.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Mode sign**: 20 % (max): Mortgage debt / GDP 0.18 on Manual, -0.03 on Automatic; Bank capital ratio 0.04 on Manual, -0.02 on Automatic.

- `fishingFee`: “No fee: profit at or below normal” instead of “–”, months 25–240; its label changed 1 time(s) in the run
- `dividendsXF`: “Profits too low to pay out” / “Owners put money in: debt above normal” instead of “–”, months 1–90; its label changed 3 time(s) in the run

## Net immigration (`netImmigration`)

*One-off, unit thousand people, default 5, range -5 to 10 in steps of 0.5.*

A wave of workers arriving from abroad (or leaving), looking for work.

**Definition.** One-off shift in the labour force, in thousands of people of working age, in the month the lever is fired (5 thousand is about 2% of the labour force). They arrive looking for work, so unemployment rises at once, mostly among the young and working age; wage growth slows through the Phillips curve and their benefits add to spending. They then find work or move on: half within about a year and a half, so the extra unemployment fades over a few years. The shift is not reversed otherwise. It does not change the migration buffer, which applies to changes in jobs.

Runs: -5 thousand people (min, -default); -2.5 thousand people (-half); 2.5 thousand people (half); 5 thousand people (default); 10 thousand people (max). A one-off fires once, before month 1.

## Migration buffer (`migration`)

*Setting, unit %, default 30, range 0 to 80 in steps of 5.*

Share of job gains or losses met by workers arriving or leaving.

**Definition.** Level of the migration share, in percent of any change in jobs, persistent while set. It acts only on changes in jobs relative to baseline, so on its own it changes nothing: it matters once another lever moves jobs, and then a new setting re-sizes the labour force at once. Setting it back to 30 restores the baseline behaviour. For a wave of workers arriving into a steady economy, use net immigration.

Runs: 0 % (min); 20 % (down); 45 % (up); 80 % (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Also run on top of a companion shock, **Foreign demand -20 %** (`foreignDemand`), because the buffer acts only on changes in jobs from the baseline, and there are none without a shock. Those runs are measured against the run with the companion shock alone, in the same mode, and follow the plain runs.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Inert**: none of its 8 runs moves a headline or an indicator by 0.005 or more in any month.

## Income-tax rate (`incomeTax`)

*Setting, unit pp, default 0, range -10 to 10 in steps of 0.5.*

Changes the average tax rate on wages, benefits and pensions, held where you set it. The debt rule only suggests a value beside the lever.

**Definition.** Level shift in the income-tax rate, in percentage points from its baseline, applied in the month it is set and held there until you change it (stabilisers on Manual). This is the whole change: the debt rule only suggests a value beside the lever. Setting it back to 0 removes your shift. It has no effect while stabilisers are Automatic, when the debt rule and your offset set the rate. With the key rate held (Manual), a lasting change that keeps unemployment off its normal rate keeps inflation off target, and the price level drifts: expectations are only half anchored to the target, so the long-run Phillips curve is not vertical (decision 0002 §6). On Automatic the central bank’s rule brings inflation back.

Runs: -10 pp (min); -2.5 pp (down); 2.5 pp (up); 10 pp (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Not run on Automatic: the lever is shown only on Manual (showWhen).

## Income tax: your offset to the rule (`incomeTaxOffset`)

*Setting, unit pp, default 0, range -10 to 10 in steps of 0.5.*

Sets the income-tax rate this many points above (or below) where the debt rule puts it.

**Definition.** Level shift in the income-tax rate, in percentage points on top of the baseline rate and the debt rule’s adjustment, applied in the month it is set and persistent while set (stabilisers on Automatic). The debt rule keeps leaning against government debt underneath it. Setting it back to 0 leaves the rate to the rule. It has no effect while stabilisers are Manual.

Runs: -10 pp (min); -2.5 pp (down); 2.5 pp (up); 10 pp (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Not run on Manual: the lever is shown only on Automatic (showWhen).

## VAT rate (`vat`)

*Setting, unit pp, default 0, range -10 to 10 in steps of 0.5.*

Changes the effective VAT rate on consumer spending; shops pass it into prices over a few months.

**Definition.** Level shift in the effective VAT rate, in percentage points, applied at once and persistent while set. VAT is paid at the new rate at once; shops pass it into their prices over a few months (about 40% in the first month, nearly all within six), keeping the difference in their margins meanwhile. Consumer prices follow, and indexed debts are revalued with them. Setting it back to 0 removes the shift (prices drop back the same way). With the key rate held (Manual), a lasting change that keeps unemployment off its normal rate keeps inflation off target, and the price level drifts: expectations are only half anchored to the target, so the long-run Phillips curve is not vertical (decision 0002 §6). On Automatic the central bank’s rule brings inflation back.

Runs: -10 pp (min); -2.5 pp (down); 2.5 pp (up); 10 pp (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Mode sign**: -10 pp (min): Investment (real) -0.39 on Manual, 0.68 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -2.5 pp (down): Investment (real) -0.11 on Manual, 0.16 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 2.5 pp (up): Investment (real) 0.04 on Manual, -0.22 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 10 pp (max): Investment (real) 0.21 on Manual, -0.85 on Automatic; Bank capital ratio 0.02 on Manual, -0.05 on Automatic.

## Health spending (`health`)

*Setting, unit % of GDP, default 0, range -3 to 3 in steps of 0.1.*

Real change in public health spending: staff pay and purchases.

**Definition.** Level shift in real health spending, % of baseline GDP a year, split between staff and purchases as at baseline; persistent while set. Nominal spending also rises with wages and prices. Setting it back to 0 returns spending to baseline; the debt built up meanwhile remains. With the key rate held (Manual), a lasting change that keeps unemployment off its normal rate keeps inflation off target, and the price level drifts: expectations are only half anchored to the target, so the long-run Phillips curve is not vertical (decision 0002 §6). On Automatic the central bank’s rule brings inflation back.

Runs: -3 % of GDP (min); -0.8 % of GDP (down); 0.8 % of GDP (up); 3 % of GDP (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Mode sign**: -3 % of GDP (min): Household consumption (real) -0.30 on Manual, 0.98 on Automatic; Investment (real) -0.88 on Manual, 0.41 on Automatic; Exports (real) -0.10 on Manual, 0.14 on Automatic; Real house prices -2.39 on Manual, 0.20 on Automatic; Profits, exporters (real) -0.03 on Manual, 5.93 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -0.8 % of GDP (down): Household consumption (real) -0.08 on Manual, 0.26 on Automatic; Investment (real) -0.23 on Manual, 0.11 on Automatic; Exports (real) -0.03 on Manual, 0.04 on Automatic; Real house prices -0.64 on Manual, 0.03 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 0.8 % of GDP (up): Household consumption (real) 0.08 on Manual, -0.26 on Automatic; Investment (real) 0.22 on Manual, -0.12 on Automatic; Exports (real) 0.03 on Manual, -0.04 on Automatic; Real house prices 0.62 on Manual, -0.06 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 3 % of GDP (max): Household consumption (real) 0.31 on Manual, -0.96 on Automatic; Investment (real) 0.84 on Manual, -0.44 on Automatic; Exports (real) 0.10 on Manual, -0.15 on Automatic; Real house prices 2.36 on Manual, -0.24 on Automatic; Bank capital ratio 0.04 on Manual, -0.03 on Automatic; and 1 more.

## Education spending (`education`)

*Setting, unit % of GDP, default 0, range -3 to 3 in steps of 0.1.*

Real change in public education spending.

**Definition.** Level shift in real education spending, % of baseline GDP a year, persistent while set, split between staff and purchases as at baseline. Setting it back to 0 returns spending to baseline. With the key rate held (Manual), a lasting change that keeps unemployment off its normal rate keeps inflation off target, and the price level drifts: expectations are only half anchored to the target, so the long-run Phillips curve is not vertical (decision 0002 §6). On Automatic the central bank’s rule brings inflation back.

Runs: -3 % of GDP (min); -0.8 % of GDP (down); 0.8 % of GDP (up); 3 % of GDP (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Mode sign**: -3 % of GDP (min): Household consumption (real) -0.52 on Manual, 0.85 on Automatic; Investment (real) -0.84 on Manual, 0.54 on Automatic; Real house prices -2.68 on Manual, 0.10 on Automatic; Government debt / GDP 0.08 on Manual, -1.11 on Automatic; Profits, domestic firms (real) -1.69 on Manual, 0.61 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -0.8 % of GDP (down): Household consumption (real) -0.14 on Manual, 0.22 on Automatic; Investment (real) -0.22 on Manual, 0.14 on Automatic; Government debt / GDP 0.02 on Manual, -0.30 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 0.8 % of GDP (up): Household consumption (real) 0.13 on Manual, -0.23 on Automatic; Investment (real) 0.21 on Manual, -0.16 on Automatic; Real house prices 0.70 on Manual, -0.04 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 3 % of GDP (max): Household consumption (real) 0.56 on Manual, -0.81 on Automatic; Investment (real) 0.80 on Manual, -0.58 on Automatic; Real house prices 2.67 on Manual, -0.14 on Automatic.

## Other public services (`otherServices`)

*Setting, unit % of GDP, default 0, range -3 to 3 in steps of 0.1.*

Real change in other public services: administration, police, culture, roads.

**Definition.** Level shift in real spending on other public services, % of baseline GDP a year, persistent while set, split between staff and purchases as at baseline. Setting it back to 0 returns spending to baseline. With the key rate held (Manual), a lasting change that keeps unemployment off its normal rate keeps inflation off target, and the price level drifts: expectations are only half anchored to the target, so the long-run Phillips curve is not vertical (decision 0002 §6). On Automatic the central bank’s rule brings inflation back.

Runs: -3 % of GDP (min); -0.8 % of GDP (down); 0.8 % of GDP (up); 3 % of GDP (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Mode sign**: -3 % of GDP (min): Investment (real) -0.93 on Manual, 0.23 on Automatic; Real house prices -1.99 on Manual, 0.34 on Automatic; Profits, exporters (real) -2.51 on Manual, 2.77 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -0.8 % of GDP (down): Investment (real) -0.25 on Manual, 0.06 on Automatic; Real house prices -0.53 on Manual, 0.08 on Automatic; Profits, exporters (real) -0.66 on Manual, 0.68 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 0.8 % of GDP (up): Investment (real) 0.24 on Manual, -0.07 on Automatic; Real house prices 0.51 on Manual, -0.10 on Automatic; Profits, exporters (real) 0.62 on Manual, -0.70 on Automatic.

- `unemployedO`: “Few unemployed left: extra jobs go to people arriving from abroad” instead of “–”, months 1–196; its label changed 2 time(s) in the run

## Public investment (`publicInvestment`)

*Setting, unit % of GDP, default 0, range -3 to 3 in steps of 0.1.*

Real change in public investment bought from domestic firms.

**Definition.** Level shift in real public investment, % of baseline GDP a year, persistent while set; nominal spending moves with domestic prices. Setting it back to 0 returns it to baseline. With the key rate held (Manual), a lasting change that keeps unemployment off its normal rate keeps inflation off target, and the price level drifts: expectations are only half anchored to the target, so the long-run Phillips curve is not vertical (decision 0002 §6). On Automatic the central bank’s rule brings inflation back.

Runs: -3 % of GDP (min); -0.8 % of GDP (down); 0.8 % of GDP (up); 3 % of GDP (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Mode sign**: -3 % of GDP (min): Real house prices -1.25 on Manual, 0.48 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -0.8 % of GDP (down): Real house prices -0.34 on Manual, 0.13 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 0.8 % of GDP (up): Real house prices 0.30 on Manual, -0.15 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 3 % of GDP (max): Real house prices 1.13 on Manual, -0.56 on Automatic.

## Old-age and disability transfers (`oldAgeTransfers`)

*Setting, unit % of GDP, default 0, range -2 to 2 in steps of 0.1.*

Real change in public pensions and disability benefits (mostly to older people).

**Definition.** Level shift in real old-age and disability transfers, % of baseline GDP a year, indexed to the CPI and split by age as at baseline; persistent while set. Setting it back to 0 ends it. With the key rate held (Manual), a lasting change that keeps unemployment off its normal rate keeps inflation off target, and the price level drifts: expectations are only half anchored to the target, so the long-run Phillips curve is not vertical (decision 0002 §6). On Automatic the central bank’s rule brings inflation back.

Runs: -2 % of GDP (min); -0.5 % of GDP (down); 0.5 % of GDP (up); 2 % of GDP (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Mode sign**: -2 % of GDP (min): Profits, exporters (real) -0.60 on Manual, 0.60 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -0.5 % of GDP (down): Profits, exporters (real) -0.15 on Manual, 0.15 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 0.5 % of GDP (up): Profits, exporters (real) 0.14 on Manual, -0.16 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 2 % of GDP (max): Profits, exporters (real) 0.56 on Manual, -0.62 on Automatic.

## Family and housing benefits (`familyBenefits`)

*Setting, unit % of GDP, default 0, range -2 to 2 in steps of 0.1.*

Real change in child, parental-leave and housing benefits (young and working age).

**Definition.** Level shift in real family and housing benefits, % of baseline GDP a year, indexed to the CPI and split by age as at baseline; persistent while set. Setting it back to 0 ends it. With the key rate held (Manual), a lasting change that keeps unemployment off its normal rate keeps inflation off target, and the price level drifts: expectations are only half anchored to the target, so the long-run Phillips curve is not vertical (decision 0002 §6). On Automatic the central bank’s rule brings inflation back.

Runs: -2 % of GDP (min); -0.5 % of GDP (down); 0.5 % of GDP (up); 2 % of GDP (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Mode sign**: -2 % of GDP (min): Profits, exporters (real) -0.67 on Manual, 0.74 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -0.5 % of GDP (down): Profits, exporters (real) -0.17 on Manual, 0.19 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 0.5 % of GDP (up): Profits, exporters (real) 0.16 on Manual, -0.19 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 2 % of GDP (max): Profits, exporters (real) 0.63 on Manual, -0.76 on Automatic.

## Unemployment-benefit rate (`unemploymentBenefits`)

*Setting, unit pp of wage, default 0, range -30 to 30 in steps of 5.*

Changes the replacement rate paid automatically to the unemployed.

**Definition.** Level shift in the replacement rate, in percentage points of the average wage, applied to everyone unemployed at once and persistent while set. It works two ways. At once, the unemployed have more to spend, which supports demand and jobs (an automatic stabiliser). Within about a year, people out of work search longer before taking a job and workers hold out for more pay, so normal unemployment rises: +30 points raises unemployment by about 0.6–0.8 points after a few years. Setting it back to 0 ends it the same way. With the key rate held (Manual), a lasting change that keeps unemployment off its normal rate keeps inflation off target, and the price level drifts: expectations are only half anchored to the target, so the long-run Phillips curve is not vertical (decision 0002 §6). On Automatic the central bank’s rule brings inflation back.

Runs: -30 pp of wage (min); -10 pp of wage (down); 10 pp of wage (up); 30 pp of wage (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

## Who buys new government bonds (`bondBuyers`)

*Choice, unit choice, default 0.*

Banks and the central bank pay with newly created money; pension funds and households pay with existing deposits.

**Definition.** Choice, persistent while set: every new bond sold from then on goes to the chosen buyer, or 40/60 to banks and pension funds in the mix. Pension funds and older households buy only what their deposits can pay for that month; banks take the rest. When the budget is in surplus the government buys bonds back from every holder in proportion to what they hold, whatever the choice. Bonds already sold stay where they are, though pension funds and older households slowly sell surplus bonds to banks to restore their portfolio shares.

Runs: Banks; Central bank; Pension funds; Older households. Each is set before month 1 and held.

Also run on top of a companion shock, **Public investment 2 % of GDP** (`publicInvestment`), because the choice acts only on new bonds, and the baseline budget balances, so none are sold without a deficit. Those runs are measured against the run with the companion shock alone, in the same mode, and follow the plain runs.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Inert**: none of its 8 runs moves a headline or an indicator by 0.005 or more in any month.

## Pension funds’ foreign allocation (`pfForeign`)

*Setting, unit pp of assets, default 0, range -20 to 20 in steps of 1.*

Shifts the target foreign share of pension assets; funds move toward it through new flows, selling krónur.

**Definition.** Level shift in the target foreign share, in percentage points of assets, persistent while set. Funds close the gap at 0.5 a year, so most of the buying happens over two to three years. Setting it back to 0 makes them sell foreign assets back toward the old share.

Runs: -20 pp of assets (min); -5 pp of assets (down); 5 pp of assets (up); 20 pp of assets (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Mode sign**: -20 pp of assets (min): Government debt / GDP 0.23 on Manual, -0.64 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -5 pp of assets (down): Government debt / GDP 0.06 on Manual, -0.16 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 5 pp of assets (up): Government debt / GDP -0.06 on Manual, 0.14 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 20 pp of assets (max): Government debt / GDP -0.21 on Manual, 0.44 on Automatic.
